========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hey, yeah, nice to meet you too. Um, so I guess I'm here because I just keep making these really dumb mistakes at work. I supervise a sales team, and I have to do all these sales reports, and lately I just keep messing them up. Like, I'll copy the exact wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I'll completely miss a line in a procedure I'm supposed to check. 

Which is crazy because the dashboard software we use is supposed to make it idiot-proof. We actually just switched to this new system right before Thanksgiving... I remember because my brother-in-law was over for dinner and he was bragging about how his company uses some fancy custom software. He's in logistics, totally different ballgame. He drives one of those massive rigs, which honestly sounds kind of nice sometimes, you know? Just being out on the open road instead of staring at a screen all day. I bought a motorcycle a few years ago thinking I'd do that on weekends, get out of the house, but it mostly just sits in the garage now. Need to get the battery replaced. 

But, uh, anyway, yeah... even when I swear I went over the reports and double-checked everything, I still somehow miss things and put the wrong numbers in. It's getting pretty embarrassing.

Yeah, actually. I mean, my mom still brings it up sometimes. She kept all these old report cards and notes from my teachers in elementary school. They always said the same thing, like, I rushed through my work, or I was constantly losing my homework. We have a Golden Retriever, Bailey, and she sheds like crazy. You can't wear black in our house, I swear.

History]\nDoctor: Outside of work, do these attention problems also cause trouble at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely spills over. Who can get there by six?\n\nDoctor: When you're in a meeting or reading something important, do you find your mind drifting off even when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's like, I'll be sitting there trying to review my team's call notes or looking at the numbers for the week, and just one conversation across the room completely derails me. Or I'll get a random thought about something at home, like whether I remembered to close the garage door. We had a raccoon get in there once because I left it open. It got into the trash cans and made a huge mess. It took me all Sunday morning to clean it up, and my wife was so mad because we were supposed to go to her parents' house for brunch. Her mom makes these incredible blueberry pancakes, she uses actual buttermilk, which I guess makes a huge difference. I tried making them once from a mix and they just weren't the same. But yeah, anyway, it's like my mind just gets pulled off track before I even realize I've stopped reading. I'll get to the bottom of a page and have no idea what it actually said.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to stay seated when you’re expected to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, constantly. It's actually really uncomfortable. Like, if I'm sitting through a long meeting, it feels like my whole body is just buzzing inside. I end up having to shift around in my chair, or I'm tapping my foot the whole time. Sometimes I just have to make up an excuse to go to the bathroom or grab water just so I can stand up. The coffee machine at work is actually broken right now, which is a nightmare. It's one of those Keurig ones, and I think someone tried to use a pod that wasn't compatible and jammed the whole thing up. Now we have to walk all the way down to the lobby cafe if we want coffee, and they charge like five dollars for a regular drip. It's ridiculous. I should probably just start bringing a thermos from home, but I never remember to make it in the morning. I bought this really nice Yeti tumbler, the dark blue one, specifically for that. Keeps it hot for hours. But yeah, I just can't sit still for very long without feeling like I need to jump out of my skin.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these focus problems happen pretty much every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah, pretty much every single day. It's just a constant thing. Like yesterday, I was trying to get through this stack of performance reviews for my team, and I just couldn't keep my eyes on the screen. Which is bad, because I really need to get them done before Friday. We're having this big team lunch on Friday at this new Italian place that opened up down the street. Supposedly they make all their pasta from scratch right there in the window. My buddy Dave went last week and said the lasagna was unbelievable, but he said parking is a total nightmare. You have to park like three blocks away and walk, which I don't mind, but you know how it is when you only have an hour for lunch. Plus, it's supposed to rain on Friday, so walking three blocks in the rain just for some lasagna seems like a lot of effort. I might just order a sandwich from the deli instead. They do this turkey club that's really good, they use that thick-cut bacon. But yeah, to answer your question, it's definitely an everyday struggle for me.

Yeah, organization is... well, honestly, I don't really know if I have trouble with it or not. I mean, my desk at work is pretty messy, but I usually know where things are, mostly. It got into the trash cans and made a huge mess. It took me all Sunday morning to clean it up, and my wife was so mad because we were supposed to go to her parents' house for brunch. Her mom makes these incredible blueberry pancakes, she uses actual buttermilk, which I guess makes a huge difference. I tried making them once from a mix and they just weren't the same. But yeah, anyway, it's like my mind just gets pulled off track before I even realize I've stopped reading. I'll get to the bottom of a page and have no idea what it actually said.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to stay seated when you’re expected to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, constantly. It's actually really uncomfortable. Like, if I'm sitting through a long meeting, it feels like my whole body is just buzzing inside. I end up having to shift around in my chair, or I'm tapping my foot the whole time. Sometimes I just have to make up an excuse to go to the bathroom or grab water just so I can stand up. The coffee machine at work is actually broken right now, which is a nightmare. It's one of those Keurig ones, and I think someone tried to use a pod that wasn't compatible and jammed the whole thing up. Now we have to walk all the way down to the lobby cafe if we want coffee, and they charge like five dollars for a regular drip. It's ridiculous. I should probably just start bringing a thermos from home, but I never remember to make it in the morning. I bought this really nice Yeti tumbler, the dark blue one, specifically for that. Keeps it hot for hours. But yeah, I just can't sit still for very long without feeling like I need to jump out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do these focus problems happen pretty much every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much every single day. Supposedly they make all their pasta from scratch right there in the window. My buddy Dave went last week and said the lasagna was unbelievable, but he said parking is a total nightmare. You have to park like three blocks away and walk, which I don't mind, but you know how it is when you only have an hour for lunch. Plus, it's supposed to rain on Friday, so walking three blocks in the rain just for some lasagna seems like a lot of effort. I might just order a sandwich from the deli instead. They do this turkey club that's really good, they use that thick-cut bacon. Her mom makes these incredible blueberry pancakes, she uses actual buttermilk, which I guess makes a huge difference. I tried making them once from a mix and they just weren't the same. But yeah, anyway, it's like my mind just gets pulled off track before I even realize I've stopped reading. The coffee machine at work is actually broken right now, which is a nightmare. It's one of those Keurig ones, and I think someone tried to use a pod that wasn't compatible and jammed the whole thing up. Now we have to walk all the way down to the lobby cafe if we want coffee, and they charge like five dollars for a regular drip. It's ridiculous. But yeah, I just can't sit still for very long without feeling like I need to jump out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do these focus problems happen pretty much every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much every single day. It's just a constant thing. Like yesterday, I was trying to get through this stack of performance reviews for my team, and I just couldn't keep my eyes on the screen. Which is bad, because I really need to get them done before Friday. We're having this big team lunch on Friday at this new Italian place that opened up down the street. Supposedly they make all their pasta from scratch right there in the window. My buddy Dave went last week and said the lasagna was unbelievable, but he said parking is a total nightmare. You have to park like three blocks away and walk, which I don't mind, but you know how it is when you only have an hour for lunch. Plus, it's supposed to rain on Friday, so walking three blocks in the rain just for some lasagna seems like a lot of effort. I might just order a sandwich from the deli instead. They do this turkey club that's really good, they use that thick-cut bacon. But yeah, to answer your question, it's definitely an everyday struggle for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, organization is... well, honestly, I don't really know if I have trouble with it or not. I mean, my desk at work is pretty messy, but I usually know where things are, mostly. It's like organized chaos, you know? My boss hates it. He's one of those guys who needs everything completely spotless. He actually came in on a Saturday once just to reorganize the supply closet because he couldn't stand how the printer paper was stacked. He's a good guy, though. He let me leave early a couple of weeks ago when my car broke down. The alternator just completely died on me right on the highway. I had to get it towed to this shop near my house, and the guy there tried to charge me like eight hundred bucks. I told him he was crazy, you can get a remanufactured one online for like a hundred and fifty. My brother-in-law is a mechanic, so I usually just have him look at stuff, but he was out of town on a fishing trip. They go up to this lake in Wisconsin every year for walleye. He caught a huge one last time, mounted it in his basement. His wife hates it, says it smells, but I think it looks pretty cool. But yeah, organization... I'm not really sure if it's a huge problem for me or just how I do things.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention problems caused serious problems in your life, like at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. It's actually gotten to the point where my manager put it in my formal review, which was really stressful. We had the review in that little glass conference room near the breakroom, the one where the AC is always blasting. I swear it's like forty degrees in there. I had to wear my heavy winter coat inside just to stop shivering. It's this North Face jacket I got on sale last Black Friday, actually a really good deal. I stood in line for like three hours outside the mall, which was crazy because it was snowing, but they were giving out those free hot chocolates from the kiosk, so it wasn't too bad. But yeah, the review was rough. And at home, it's causing issues too, because people feel like they can't count on me to follow through on things. It just adds to the conflict, you know? Like my sister was supposed to come over for dinner on Sunday, and she got all mad because she said I always forget to pick up the stuff she likes. She's doing this keto diet right now, which seems impossible to me. She's eating like, blocks of cheese and bacon, but no bread. I couldn't live without bread. There's this bakery near my house that makes these fresh sourdough loaves on weekend mornings, and the smell is just incredible. But yeah, it's definitely causing some real problems for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused during things you do for fun, like watching a movie or reading at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if I have trouble focusing on fun stuff. I guess it depends on what it is. Like, if I'm watching a movie, sometimes I'll pull out my phone and start scrolling, but everyone does that now, right? My wife and I were trying to watch that new superhero movie on streaming the other night, the one with the guy from that old sitcom. He looks so different now, I think he got hair plugs or something. Which is fine, no judgment, but it just looks a little unnatural around the hairline, you know? Anyway, we were watching it, and the dog started whining to go out. We have this golden retriever mix, Buster, and he's got this stomach thing where he can only eat this super expensive prescription food. We tried switching him to a cheaper brand once, and it was a disaster. I had to rent one of those carpet cleaners from the grocery store. Have you ever used one of those? They're incredibly heavy, and getting it into the trunk of my sedan was a whole workout. I really need to get an SUV, especially with winter coming. My buddy just bought a used Tahoe and he loves it, says it handles the snow like a champ. But yeah, for fun stuff... I don't know, I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether I lose focus or not.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention or restlessness problems show up in more than one place, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I was definitely like this as a kid, too. My mom actually still brings it up. She kept all these old report cards and notes from my teachers in elementary school, and they all said the same thing—that I'd rush through my work, lose my homework, talk out of turn, and just couldn't stay in my seat without them constantly reminding me. She keeps them in this giant plastic tub in her basement, right next to her sewing machine. She's super into quilting right now. It's wild, she spends hours matching up these tiny little squares of fabric. She made me this huge quilt for my bed last Christmas, all in these dark blue and gray colors. It's really warm, actually, maybe a little too warm. I had to kick it off the bed in the middle of the night last week because I woke up sweating. I usually sleep with a fan on, even in the winter, just for the white noise. I bought this tower fan from Costco a few years ago, and it has this little remote control that I always lose in the couch cushions. Our couch is one of those deep, squishy sectionals, and it just eats everything. I found like three dollars in quarters and a dog toy down there the other day. But yeah, the stuff at school and home... it's been happening since I was little, for sure."

Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHey, yeah, nice to meet you too. Um, so I guess I'm here because I just keep making these really dumb mistakes at work. I supervise a sales team, and I have to do all these sales reports, and lately I just keep messing them up. Like, I'll copy the exact wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I'll completely miss a line in a procedure I'm supposed to check. \n\nWhich is crazy because the dashboard software we use is supposed to make it idiot-proof. We actually just switched to this new system right before Thanksgiving... I remember because my brother-in-law was over for dinner and he was bragging about how his company uses some fancy custom software. He's in logistics, totally different ballgame. He drives one of those massive rigs, which honestly sounds kind of nice sometimes, you know? Just being out on the open road instead of staring at a screen all day. I bought a motorcycle a few years ago thinking I'd do that on weekends, get out of the house, but it mostly just sits in the garage now. Need to get the battery replaced. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, yeah... even when I swear I went over the reports and double-checked everything, I still somehow miss things and put the wrong numbers in. It's getting pretty embarrassing.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Plus, it's supposed to rain on Friday, so walking three blocks in the rain just for some lasagna seems like a lot of effort. I might just order a sandwich from the deli instead.
